[trialAccount t18580405-468]
468. JOHN NETHERCOTE (26) , Stealing, 1 metal plane, 3 pieces of gun metal, 40 pieces of wood, and 2lbs. weight of screws, value 1 l. 6 s. 6 d.; the goods of our Lady the Queen, his mistress.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY MORRIS . I am master wheeler in the Royal Arsenal carriage department, at Woolwich—the prisoner was employed there as a wheelwright since last Nov.—on 18th March, I found this metal plane secreted under the prisoner's work bench—it was suspended under the board which projects below the bench—the prisoner, the next day, was reported to Colonel Tulloch—thisplane was found on Thursday, and it was produced and shown to the prisoner, when he was taken before Colonel Tulloch, on the Monday morning—theprisoner acknowledged making it, but said it was for the use of the shop—I engaged the prisoner myself—I engage all the men, and the engagement is that they should find their own tools—they are not allowed to make tools out of Government property to work with—nothing was said to him about finding his own tools—it is not said directly to every man, but it is a standing rule in the place—a man cannot go to work till he brings his tools—theprisoner was told to come to work—I cannot swear that he was told to bring his tools—it is the practice of the wheelers when they come to work to bring their tools—the prisoner brought his tools with him—he had no right to use the Government property to make a plane to work with—this plane is worth about 1 l.; the metal is worth about 15 s.—after the inquiry the prisoner was suspended from his work—he then packed up three planes, and was going to take them out—he would have taken them out if I had allowed him to pass the gate—on examining them I found the mouth of the planes were bushed with brass, which, is a piece of metal let into the edge—I detained the prisoner on account of the brass being in the mouth of the planes—I told him so—I do not think he said anything—the pieces of metal were taken out of the planes; these are them—they are gun metal; such metal as is in use in the department—he had no right to have this metal in his planes—these chisel handles are of a kind of wood which is used for the greater part of the wheels of carriages fitted out for China—the prisoner had no right to have this.
Cross-examined by MR. COOPER. Q. Were the places in which these bits of gun-metal were used, in the government department? A. Yes; after the prisoner had been suspended, he came for his wages—I did not know where he was lodging—person's characters are not inquired after before they are employed—other workmen have not put those pieces to mend their planes to my knowledge—I have examined them since—I suppose the value of these wooden handles is about 1 d. each—the materials of this plane are worth about 15 s.—the prisoner was suspended on Monday, the 22d March.
JURY. Q. Has this one plane been used? A. I should imagine it has been used, just to try the working of it, I do not think any more—there is a printed notice put up in the shop—no man is allowed to repair a tool without giving me notice.
COURT. Q. But is it said that they must not make any tool? A. Yes, strictly forbidding it.
JAMES CLARK . I live in Broomfield Place, Plumstead. I am the foreman of the wheel factory at the Arsenal. The prisoner is employed there—Iwas present when this plane was found on the Thursday, and on the Monday following I found three pieces of gun-metal at the prisoner's bench—theywere in the rack in his bench—I took them to the
last witness—the prisoner had no right to have these pieces there at all—he was boxing wood for the gun carriages, the last few days he was working—he was making powder barrels—he would use some metal, but no gun-metal whatever.
Cross-examined. Q. Where do those men lodge? A. In Woolwich, some of them—I believe the prisoner lodged there, but I did not find him—hewould not us such a plane as this for powder barrels—I never saw a plane like this, I would not use it for wood—I have planed, but I have a wooden tool—I never saw a plane of this description before—I never heard that there was any quarrel with the other men and the prisoner about his not paying his footing.
JOHN SARGEANT ( Policeman, R 107). I am on duty at the Royal Arsenal, at Woolwich. On Tuesday night, 23d March, I took the prisoner into custody, at hit lodging, at Woolwich—I told him it was on a charge of stealing three pieces of gun-metal, value 10 d.—I showed him these pieces, which I received from Mr. Morris, the master wheeler—he said he had bought them in Petticoat Lane, and he afterwards said that two of the pieces were the property of the Crown, and one piece he had bought—I searched a box which was in the back yard; he said the box was his, and all that it contained—I found in it twenty-six pieces of wood of this description ( produced), and fourteen smaller pieces of another sort of wood—I asked him how he came by this wood—he said he bought it in Woolwich; I asked him if, when he bought it in Woolwich, it was an entire piece of wood? he said it was—I asked hint where in Woolwich he bought it—he said he did not know—he afterwards said it was three pieces of wood that these were made of, and that he had them of a man for whom he was mending a chest of drawers and that the man's name was Bowden, who was employed in the carriage department as a fitter—I searched, his room, and found about 11b. 9oz. of brass screws, which were marked with the broad arrow, and about 2lbs. of iron screws—the prisoner was not present, when I found these.
Cross-examined. Q. There isa man named Bowden? A. Yes, he is employed in the Arsenal department—he is not here.
( The prisoner received a good character.)
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18580405-496]
496. JOHN NETHERCOTE was again indicted for stealing 1 ring engine, value 7 s. 6 d.; the goods of George Fredrick Young and others, his masters.
MR. LILLEY conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL COLE . I am storekeeper in the employ of George Frederick Young and others; they are ship builders, at Poplar. The prisoner was in their employ last Sept. twelve months—to the best of my knowledge he was there about six months, employed as a shipwright; it was at the time of the strike—amongstother tools in my charge, there was a ring engine—the workmen give their names to me when they want anything from the stores; they supply their own tools, but supposing any extraordinary tool is wanted, they apply to their employer for it, and this ring engine is such a tool—I have looked at it it is the property of George Frederick Young and Co.—while the prisoner was in their employ he had access to such tools as this—about the time of his leaving there were several tools missing, and one or two of these articles.
Cross-examined by MR. COOPER. Q. When he left, did they give him a certificate of good character? A. As far as I know, they did—this instrument is perfect with the exception of a handle which is put in here; it is worth 7 s. 6 d.—it would be of no use, but for one purpose—I know this as I have have had it in my possession several times, and you will see a mark on it—I have had no occasion to use it, but I have given it out—I had not seen
this for a considerable time; not for about eighteen months before it was shown to me—we had only thirty-two of these—they are given to the men to work with—the prisoner left the employ last Sept. twelvemonths; I put the names down, and the day of the month when they leave; I have not the book here—I do not put down to whom the tools are lent; I put the name of the tool on a slate; I have the slate but not the name down—I have not lent a great many tools in the last four or five months.
MR. LILLEY. Q. Did you ever lend one of these tools to the prisoner? A. No, I did not; he had no right to remove one of these tools.
COURT. Q. You say you missed some tools when he left; were any inquiries made about them? A. I am the person to make inquiries—I report it.
JOHN SARGEANT ( Policeman, R 107). On Tuesday, 23d March, I apprehended the prisoner at his house, at Woolwich—I found three boxes containing tools; the prisoner said the boxes and the contents were his—one of them contained nine saws; in one of them I found this tool—Mr. Cole selected it when the boxes and tools were taken to the Arsenal, for the workmen to inspect them—I was present when Mr. Cole selected this—this was the only one of this sort that I found.
GUILTY .— Recommended to mercy by the Jury.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18580405-470]
470. JOHN NETHERCOTE was again indicted for stealing on 15th Jan., 1 spokeshave, value 1 s., the property of Frederick Gardner.
MR. LILLEY conducted the Prosecution.
FREDERICK GARDNER . I am a wheeler employed in the Royal Arsenal—Ihave been employed there about fifteen years—I was there in the beginning of the present year—the prisoner was employed there at the same time; he came there after me—since the beginning of the year I have missed a spokeshave; it is somewhere about three months ago, but I cannot say exactly the date—I had last seen it on my bench—the prisoner worked two benches from me—after I missed it I made inquiries—I asked all round the shop—it was not restored to me—I next saw it produced by a policeman in the waiting room at the Arsenal gates; that was about the 24th March—I never lent it to the prisoner; I have a distinct recollection that I did not—this ( produced) is my spokeshave—I had seen it safe two days before I missed it.
Cross-examined by MR. COOPER. Q. Were you always so positive about not having lent it? A. Yes; I never expressed a doubt, and said I could could not recollect—I never said I might or might not have lent it; it was so long ago—the prisoner was expected back to the yard—I had lent tools to other workmen, but I had never lent that spokeshave—I have had some lent to me; that is a thing constantly done—I never found amongst my own tools, the tools of other workmen which I had forgotten—I never did, I swear—the value of this is about one shilling—I cannot say whether I asked the prisoner about it; I inquired all round the shop—I cannot say whether it was in his hearing—when the work is going on there is a good deal of noise—the prisoner worked two benches off; I believe I spoke in his hearing—Ikeep my tools in a drawer—when the prisoner came, there was quarrelling between him and the men about not paying his footing—I did not hear them say they would do for his business in consequence.
MR. LILLEY. Q. When he does not pay his footing, is he not "Sent to Coventry?" A. It is so at times; we do not lend our tools to a man who has not paid his footing.
COURT. Q. Do you know whether the prisoner lost any tools of value while there? A. I heard him say he lost a rule—I cannot say how long the
prisoner had been working there—it was only some months—the dispute about paying the footing was some time after he had been there.
JOHN SARGEANT ( Policeman, R 107). I was on duty at the Woolwich Arsenal on the 23d March, and I apprehended the prisoner at his house in Wensley Street, Woolwich—in the room I found some boxes containing tools—there were three boxes containing small tools, and one long one contaning saws—the prisoner said they were his—I found among the tools this spokeshave ( produced)—the other tools found were all laid out in a room at the Arsenal, in order that the workmen might come and select any they could identify—the prosecutor came and he identified this.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not the conduct of the prisoner quite open, and did he not say the tools were his? A. Yes; he put no impediment in the way of my searching—the prosecutor said nothing at all to me about lending the tools.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18580405-471]
471. JOHN NETHERCOTE was again indicted for stealing 1 chisel, value 1 s., the property of James King.
MR. LILLEY conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES KING . I am a wheeler, working in the Royal Arsenal. The prisoner was employed in the same capacity—I have been employed there about two years; the prisoner came early in November last—I worked at a bench in the shop at first—I left that, and went to work at a machine—the prisoner suceeded me at my bench—my tools were left in a chest under the bench—Idid not, at any time, miss any of my tools—I had no occasion to use them when I went to the machine; and I continued to work there—on 24th March, from information I received from the foreman, I went where some tools were spread out—I there saw this chisel ( produced)—I know it by a burn on the handle—I have no doubt about it, I have had it for seven years—Inever lent it to the prisoner, I am sure; I never lent him any tools—after I saw the chisel, I went and examined my chest—I found a chisel missing.
Cross-examined by MR. COOPER. Q. Had there not been some dispute about paying the footing? A. I know nothing about it; I know this chisel by the burn on the handle—I am quite sure of it—I have many valuable tools in my chest; I have many more chisels; that is the only one I missed—Ido not know how many tools there are in my box; there are plenty more than this—I have some valuable planes; but that is all that was taken away—Ihave never taken other men's tools, when they have been close at hand, and I wanted them—I was always on good terms with the prisoner—I did not know he was coming back; I did not trouble myself about it.
JOHN SARGEANT ( Policeman, R 107). I apprehended the prisoner on 23d March—I searched the room where I apprehended him—I found that chisel in one of the boxes; it was taken to a room in the Arsenal.
The prisoner's statement before the Magistrate was read as follows: "I never stole it; I borrowed it."
NOT GUILTY .
Before Michael Prendergast, Esq., Q.C.
[trialAccount t18580405-472]
472. HENRY DAY (23) , Feleniously wounding James Russell, with intent to resist his lawful apprehension and detainer. 2d COUNT, with intent to do grievous bodily harm.
JAMES RUSSELL ( Policeman, R 282). On Wednesday morning, 10th March, I was on duty in the Greenwich Road—I was in uniform—I looked into a gateway in front of Maitland House; it was a sort of shrubbery—I saw the
prisoner there upon his hands and knees—he was about ten feet inside the fence from the road, and about two or three feet from the house—on seeing me, he crawled into the shrubbery, and hid himself behind a tree—I could not get in at the gate, where I looked in; it was fastened—I went round to a side gate, and then went up to him—I asked him what business he had there, and he made no answer—I told him he must go to the police station—I then took hold of him, and lifted him up—we then went out at the gate, and proceeded fifty or sixty yards along the road; during that time neither of us spoke—he then suddenly struck me a violent blow on the head with this instrument ( A life preserver, produced)—the blow nearly stunned me, and cut my head open nearly two inches long—I received two other blows from him before I could secure him; one behind the ear, a cut nearly an inch long, and the other a bruise on the other side of the head—I kept up, and held him, and as soon as I sufficiently recovered from the blows, I threw him to the ground—myhair was matted with blood, and there was a considerable quantity of blood on the ground in the morning—we were on the ground together for a quarter of an hour; he tried all he could to injure me, by kicking and biting, and endeavouring to use his life preserver—I then managed to spring my rattle, and two of my brother constables came up to my assistance—I then gave him in charge—when I got to the station, I found this knife ( produced) upon him—it is used for getting open windows, in forcing back the catch of the sash—I have been in the police force for five years.
Prisoner. Q. You struck me, did you not? A. Yes—you were on the ground when I sprung my rattle—I struck you then.
WILLIAM CROUCH ( Policeman, R 332). On the 10th March, between 3 and 4 o'clock in the morning, I heard the springing of a rattle—I went in the direction of the sound in the Greenwich Road—I saw the prisoner lying upon the ground, and the policeman upon the top of him, holding him down—the prisoner had hold of the constable's rattle, and was trying to wrench it from his hand—I secured the rattle, and assisted in taking the prisoner to the station—the constable's hat was off, and his head was bleeding—on the way to the station, the prisoner was very violent, and tried to get away—there were three of us to take him; Russell was able to help—on his way, he said, "You b—s, I could eat better men than you"—he also said, "This comes of going to see a girl; she would have me there."
JAMES RUSSELL re-examined. I did not see any girl there—there was no light about the house—I did not see any one running away.
EDWARD MAY . I am gardener to Mr. Henry Torr, the owner of Maitland House. I do not live on the premises, but in a cottage close by—I am on the premises every day—the prisoner had no business whatever on the premises—I do not know him at all—the family were at home on the night in question.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been out drinking, and towards 12 o'clock at night, I got into company with a young woman, and she took me to this place where the constable took me; I waited there until he took me in charge, when he handled me very roughly, and I, being the worse for drink, made resistance; he directly took out his rattle, and hit me across the head; he hit me before I hit him, or I should not hate hit him.
GUILTY .
( The prisoner was further charged with having been before convicted.)
THOMAS JONES ( Policeman H 174). I produce a certificate—( Read:"Central Criminal Court, April, 1856; William Tibbert, Convicted, upon his own confession, of burglary; Imprisoned one year.")—I apprehended the prisoner on that charge, and was present at his trial—I am sure he is the same person.
GUILTY.— Six Years Penal Servitude.
[trialAccount t18580405-473]
473. WILLIAM HAMMOND (24) , Stealing 1 gown, value 13 s., the property of James Knight .
MR. PLATT conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH KNIGHT . I am the wife of James Knight, of No. 17, Church Street, Deptford; he is a shoemaker. About the end of May, 1856; I had a blue dress in my possession—this ( produced) is it—I missed it from a clothes horse in my bed room, early on Monday morning—I had worn it on the Sunday—my house is about 200 yards from the house of Mrs. Cox—on the 16th March last I saw Mrs. Cox coming down the street, and she had my dress on—I made some inquiries—I know it is my dress by the make of it, and also by the pattern—the sleeves are joined in a peculiar way, and the dressmaker pointed them out to me when she brought the dress home—I am certain it is my dress—it was quite new when I lost it—I bought it at a linen draper's, in Deptford—Mrs. Griffiths made it—she lives at Deptford—she is not here.
Cross-examined by MR. LAXTON. Q. Was Mrs. Griffiths examined before the Magistrate? A. No; she was there, but she was not required, she would have been here if she knew she was wanted—I had not worn my dress three hours—I missed it two years ago—it was on the Monday morning—I do not know whether the door was locked—it was in my bed room—I live in the house—I did not miss it when I went to bed on the Sunday night—I did not look for it—I was in the house all the time until I missed it, but I was in another room—the door was looked when we went to bed—I did not miss anything else—there were other clothes upon the horse—there were my husband's best coat and a new waistcoat—they were not taken—some part of the house is let to lodgers.
COURT. Q. Who are the parties? A. Very respectable people—two of them are married, and there is one single woman—I believe she was out at the time I missed the dress—I do not know whether the door was fastened; the key was left in, but I do not know whether it was locked or not—I do not think it was; my little boy was ill, and I went in and out several times—welive in the parlour at the back of the shop—the room from which I lost the dress is on the ground floor of the next house—we keep a shoemaker's and broker's shop, and there are two houses together—one is a private house—mybed room is in that house—there is a door between the two houses, but that is not open—there is a kitchen at the back, common to both houses, and you can go through that from one house to the other without going out into the street—I do not know whether the door of the private house was shut; it might have been open—a person might have opened that door and walked into my room and taken away the dress—my boy who was ill was lying in the room.
MARY COX . I am a widow, living at No. 87, New Church Street, Bermondsey. About the month of May or June, 1856, I lived within 200 yards of Mrs. Knight—at that time the prisoner rented some stable belonging to me—I remember about that time his leaving a bundle, to be taken care of, there were one or two sacks and a dress rolled up—the dress was not put into the sack, but the back had been put down, and the dress rolled in it; the sack was empty, and formed a wrapper—I do not think the dress could be seen from the outside—I took care of the things—from something my little girl told me, I looked at the sack, and I then saw the dress—I spoke to Charlotte Parker about it, she saw it, and she went at my request to the prisoner—she brought me back a message; in consequence of that I gave her 11 s. to give to the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. LAXTON. Q. What is the prisoner? A. A dustman, I believe—he kept two horses and carts, and at one time a chaise; I
thought him a respectable man—I had known him for about six months—I believe he kept horses and carts at Lewisham before he came to me—he paid me 2 s. a week for the stables—I used to get the rent somehow—he remained there some time after he brought the bundle, I think for seven or eight months—the bundle was there only a day or two—the prisoner did not propose that I should buy the dress; I said I would buy it, if it were for sale—hedealt in various ways as well as a dustman—I never heard that he was a dealer in marine stores; I have known him buy and sell a chaise, cart, and things of that kind—I do not know whether the sacks were ordinary corn sacks—I do not know who untied the bundle—I dare say I wanted to see—Isent Charlotte Parker to ask him whether he would sell it—I did not buy any other dress at the time—I am quite sure that is the same dress—I did not buy another for a long time after that.
SARAH ANN SLADE . About the month of May, 1856, I was living with Mrs. Cox—I was present when the prisoner left a bundle; the bundle was tied round in a sack and a handkerchief—I looked, and saw a blue dress, I can only speak to the dress by the colour—this is the dress my mistress wore continually—I afterwards saw my mistress give some money to Charlotte Parker.
Cross-examined. Q. Did the prisoner leave the bundle with you or your mistress? A. With me—he merely told me to take care of it; he did not ask me to put it anywhere—I could see the dress without untying the bundle—Icould see there was a blue dress, and that made me look.
CHARLOTTE PARKER . I am a single woman, living at No. 2, King Street, Deptford. I remember, about the time in question, seeing Mrs. Cox at the door of her house; she showed me a dress—that is it—I knew the prisoner before this—in consequence of what Mrs. Cox said, I went to him; I asked him whether he wanted to sell the drees—he said, "No," he was going to make it a present to some one; he then said if I wanted it, I might have it for 11 s.—the bargain was ultimately struck for that sum—the dress was for Mrs. Cox—she afterwards gave me the money, and I took it to the prisoner, and paid him for it.
ALFRED JOHN CROUCH ( Policeman, R 92). On 17th March I took the prisoner into custody—I said to him, "I have seen Mrs. Cox, and she says that you sold her a dress for 11 s. about twenty months ago, and that dress was stolen from Mrs. Knight's, in Church Street—he said, "I know nothing about it"—onhis way to the station, he turned round, laughingly, and said, "You know that was not the only thing stolen at that time"—I then cautioned him that he need not say anything, but anything he did say I should repeat.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you quite sure he said "stolen," and not "lost?" A. No, "stolen"—he said stolen " about that time," not " at that time;"that is the only difference.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18580405-474]
474. JAMES REYNOLDS , Stealing 1 metal tap and 4 feet of metal pipe; the property of Mary Parker.
MR. PLATT conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD JAMES WOODCOCK . I am an architect, living at Beulah Lodge, Forest Hill. I have charge of two houses there belonging to Mary Parker, called Belle Vue Villas; they have been unoccupied for some time—some time back I introduced the prisoner to a Mr. Unwin, who is the builder of some houses close by—as the architect, I gave the prisoner the privilege of going into one of the houses to take his meals—on 14th Feb., from information I received, I went to the adjoining house; No. 3, I believe it is—I then
found some lead pipe had been taken away—a piece of leaden pipe, used for the supply of water to the house No. 3, which had passed under the flooring and turned up at the back of the house, had been cut away just below the brick work which formed the paving of the yard—I had seen it safe about three weeks, I think, before that—the lead ( produced) appears to be of the same length and size as that taken away—the ends appear to be quite bright, but they are not so bright as when they were exhibited at the police court—Ishould think it had been cut by a carpenter's chisel or a very sharp instrument—thislead ( another piece) appears to correspond with the other.
JOHN ALLEN . I am a marine store dealer. On 20th Feb., I was going round Forest Hill with a pony and cart—the prisoner came out of a beer shop—he had some lead pipe with him—he asked me if I would buy it—I asked him if it was his own; he replied "Yes;" and he said he was going to Peckham Rye to sell it—I said I would buy it, if he would give me a receipt, with his name and address—he did so; and I bought it—I gave 1 s. 6 d. for it—he gave me the address, "James Reynolds, Sydenham"—a police constable afterwards came and took possession of the lead; this is it.
Cross-examined by MR. LAXTON. Q. When did you buy this? A. On Saturday morning, the 20th, about 9 o'clock—that is the way I transact my business, going about with my horse and cart—there was party there that told me "James Reynolds, Sydenham," was the correct address—he was the nephew of the landlord of the beer shop—I did not know the prisoner before—thistook place on the high road at Forest Hill—he came out of the beer-shop as I was going by—there were two or three people there besides the prisoner and myself; they are not here—I live at Deptford—that lead is the piping usually employed for water piping—I believe it is "three-quarter" pipe; the prisoner had no other pipe, nor any other old building materials.
GEORGE SIMS ( Policeman, R 229). On 20th February, at about half-past 10 o'clock, I took the lead which has been produced by the witness Allen—fromenquiries I made, I went to a public house at Peckham—I there saw the prisoner—I told him I wanted him concerning some lead; and I asked him where he got it from—he said it waa his own—I then took him to the station—the lead was here shown him; he repeated that it was his own—he said something about repairs being done to his house; he said he had a right to do as he liked with his own—I then went to the house at Forest Hill, No.3, Belle Vue Villas—I found some lead had been removed from the back part of the house; the cutting, was quite fresh—I cut off this other piece of lead from that remaining—I have compared it, and it fits exactly with the rest.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you not know of the prisoner having lived for the last two years in one place? A. I did not know anything of him before—Inever heard of a charge being made against him before—he said it was his own lead—he said something about its having been taken from his own house; that there had been some repairs—he told me where his house was; in the Wells Road—I went there and saw the sister—she told me they had not any such piping—there had been no repairs done there to my knowledge—Icould not find any place where the pipe fitted—I did not look to see if any water pipe had been repaired; I did not see any place where a pipe had been, but was there no longer—I do not know anything about any repairs having been done at the house; I do not know who keeps the house—Ibelieved the prisoner lived with his two sister—I have not made any further enquiries—I understood he lived in the Wells Road; and I found that to be the fact.
The prisoner's statement before the Magistrate was read as follows: "It
is my own; I took it out of a drain at my sister's house, where she is now living."
RICHARD JAMES WOODCOCK re-examined by MR. LAXTON. Q. You introduced the prisoner to take contracts? A. Yes; he has taken contracts under me—I am not aware that he has under any body else—he employs men under him—he has been living in the neighbourhood between nine and ten years—he has been carrying on his business as a carpenter, and taking contracts—he bore a high character, or I should not have recommended him—Iam not aware that he has a shop in the Wells Road; his sisters live there—Ido not know, of my own knowledge, that there have been repairs there; the prisoner told me so, and his sisters corroborated it.
COURT. Q. This lead corresponds exactly to that left on the house, does it not? A. It does—I have known instances of two pieces of lead being brought, in which it was difficult to say they exactly matched—I have no doubt those pieces are a match.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18580405-475]
475. OLIVER ATKINS MART , Unlawfully obtaining, by false pretences,15 s. from William Nichols.—Two other COUNTS, Obtaining 6 s. and 8 s.
MR. BAYLEY conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM NICHOLS . I am a tailor, residing in the Old Kent Road. On Monday, 15th Feb., I saw the prisoner in Gnildball Yard—at that time a woman of the name of Spurway was indebted to me in the sum of 4 l. 5 s. 3 d.—Iattended as a witness—the prisoner subpoenaed me on the previous Saturday, in an action which was to be tried there—he was recommended to me as an attorney—I had some conversation about this debt which was due—I told him a person owed me a small amount, and she had said she would pay me if it ever lay in her power, but I could not take any proceedings against her, because I had taken some goods back, reducing the amount of the bill from 9 l. 14 s. to 4 l. 5 s. 3 d.—the prisoner said, "Oh yes, that is to be got easily enough; that is all right enough"—he said, "I will call upon you this evening, to see what the amount is"—he did call; he then said, "Proceedings must be taken in the Proctor's Court to recover the debt," as the debtor was a widow, and had not administered—I afterwards saw him at the Five Bells public house in the evening—he said he had been to see Mrs. Spurway—he said she had stated that she could pay the debt, but would not pay it without giving me further trouble—I said, "That is very strange;"and he said I should have to take proceedings in the Proctor's Court, and that immediately, because he said she had stated, she had paid everybody else, but would not pay me—he had previously told me he should require 30 s. to take these proceedings, and that would be the expense of the Court—he asked me if I could advance him 15 s. towards those proceedings—Itold him I would let him know in the morning, and he left me his address; that was No. 11, Douglas Street, Deptford—I saw him on the following morning—I then paid him 15 s.—that was in consequence of what he had said before—I stated, when I gave him the 15 s. that that was to enable him to take proceedings against Mrs. Spurway in the Proctor's Court—I think he said it would soon be got, or something to that effect—I came with him to London on the next Wednesday—Iafterwards found we came to Chancery Lane—I went into a court with him—I swore to something, and paid 1 s.—the prisoner was by at the time—Ialso went to what I have found was the Court of Common Pleas Office—he then asked me to advance him summons—he told me Mrs. Spurway had been served with one summons, but he must have a second
summons to bring her before the Judge to show cause why she refused to pay when she paid the other creditors—I saw the prisoner again on Thursday; I had some conversation with him then—he sent for me from the Five Bells tavern to say a gentleman wished to see me; I went over and found it was him; he said he had seen Mrs. Spurway, and she wished to put a stop to the proceedings; I asked in what way; he said she proposed to pay 1 l. in fourteen days, and 5 s. a week until the debt and expenses were paid—he said the money must be paid into court—he did not say what court, but I understood the court in which the proceedings had been taken—I said I had no objection to take the money in that way—he said, to save us the trouble and expense again, in case she omitted any payment, we must have a Judge's order, and that would cost 8 s.—I asked him if we could not do without that, and said, "Don't you think if Mrs. Spurway's father was to be answerable, it would save the expense of a Judge's order?" and he said, "No, a Judge's order must be sighed," and then, after my solicitor was empowered; in case she omitted any one payment, to put in a broker on the following day—I then agreed to advance him the 8 s. and gave it to him—on the following Thursday morning he drew a paper to state the amount of the debt and the costs he paid—Isaw him on the following day, Friday; he made a claim for 2 l. costs; he said that was for costs incurred in the proceedings; 32 s. 6 d. for office fees; and 7 s. 6 d. his own charge—I did not pay him anything—nothing was said on that occasion about paying the instalment eight days—on Friday I made this proposal to him, I would pay part of the expenses, and did he think Mrs. Spurway would pay the money with fourteen days, and not to interfere with the Judge's order, or any other proceeding takes against Mrs. Spurway—he asked me what I would be towards the expenses; I said, "Do you think 10 s. would do?" he said, "No, that would not induce her;" I said, "I will be half the expenses, that will be 1 l.;" he said, he would go and see Mrs. Spurway—Iafterwards saw Mrs. Spurway—I made inquries about the prisoner—Igave him into custody on the next Wednesday—he never accounted to me for any money he received—I never gave him any authority to receive any money from Mrs. Spurway—he told me on Friday morning he had served Mrs. Spurway with a Judged order.
Prisoner. Q. Were you not witness in the case of Ashton v Steel? A. Yes; you subpoenaed me—I do not know anything about asking you for the expenses—Miller and Son's address was on the back of the subpoena—I did not know you were not a solicitor, I believed you to be the attorney in the case—you told me if I called on Miller and Son they would pay me—I saw you attending court at the trial with Mr. Miller—I did not suppose you were a clerk—on the morning of the trial I met a witness in the cause, of the name of Wells, I mentioned to him about Mrs. Spurway; I told him that a person owed me a little money, and I did not know is what way I could get it; was there any way of recovering it?—I did not ask him if he thought you could get in the money; I swear I did not say so—I asked him if he could tell me whether there was any means of getting this money—I did not, upon the representation of Wells, come to you, while you were dining, and state the whole circumstances, and ask you whether you would undertake to collect the debt; I came to ask you whether there was any means of getting the money—I did not tell you I had already sent two men there, and they were not able to get it—I did not tell you at the time I was speaking to you about it, that they could not get it, and that I would give you 30 s. to do so—yousaid it could be got easily enough—you did not state you were merely a clerk; you said you would consider whether you would have any thing to do
with the case—I said I had sent one person; you stated you would call in the evening, you did so; you then arranged to call on Mrs. Spurway—I saw you go over there—I did not before you left my house say a second time I would give you 30 s.; I never mentioned 30 s.; you said that would be the expense of the Proctor's Court.
Q. Were not these the words you used before I went over, that you had employed two separate men to get the money from her, but they were "duffers," and would not stick to her? A. No, I did not; I told you I had spoken to one man, who was a debt collector, but I had not received any answer, and you said you could soon get the money, but it was no use to put it into a debt collector's hands—I did not say I intended to serve her out, and if you would stick to her, I would give you 30 s., whether you got it or not—I did not call at your house the next morning, and urge you to go again and see her—I called upon you, because you asked me to advance you 15 s., and I came to tell you I would advance you 15 s. towards the proceedings—Imet Wells as I was coming down to your house—I did not ask him to come with me to your house—I met him at New Cross; he was going towards London; I did not ask him to turn back and come, he proposed to come—I asked you what the expenses would be, and you said you would guarantee they would not be more than 30 s.—you did not tell me you would not act unless under the advice of a solicitor, and that it would be necessary to have a solicitor—I did not urge you then to undertake the matter, and say I would give you 30 s. for your trouble—I did not say, "If you will come up to my house I will give you 15 s. now"—I advanced you 15 s.—Wellswas there—that was not as a part of the payment you were to receive; it was to take proceedings in the Proctor's Court—I did not tell you that the collector told me I must administer before I could go into the County Court—I did not say before the Magistrate at Greenwich, that I knew you were not a solicitor—after you first called on Mrs. Spurway, you told me she said she could pay the money, but would not pay without giving me further trouble—I did not tell you that she said she had been told she was not liable, as she had not administered—she told me herself she had not administered—I know a man named Fogo, who attends the Greenwich Police Court—he did not advise me to take these proceedings against you—Iwas sent to him by the Police to ascertain if you were a solicitor—I never heard his name mentioned other than by you—he told me since that you were to have been articled to him, but that you were not a solicitor—Wells never called on me on the Friday the money was received; nor in the evening; I did not see him on that day—I did not say to him, on his calling,"Wells, Mart has got the money, he has done wonders, I am perfectly satisfied with all he has done, and he is going to call on me to-morrow to receive some further instructions, and I am going to pay him?" I swear I stated to him on Saturday, when he asked me how I got on with Mrs. Spurway, that the money was to be paid within eight days, and you were to get a Judge's order, and receive the money on that day week—you did not, when you received the money, come over and tell me.
COURT. Q. Did you get any of the money yourself? A. No, I did not.
Prisoner. Q. What did I say I was to do with the 6 s.? A. You told me you wanted it for a second summons to bring Mrs. Spurway before the Judge, to show cause why she objected to pay my debt when she had paid the others—I did not know it was Chancery Lane where I went with you—I went afterwards, before I gave you into custody, to the two places where I swore the affidavit, to see if any proceedings had been taken—Fogo suggested
I should do that—I saw you go into a shop to purchase two pieces of paper—thatwas after I swore the affidavit—you did not ask me to lend 1 s.—Wellswas not there—I understood the papers were to take out a summons—yousaid, "Come along, we can go to work now"—I do not know that Fogo told me it was a writ and copy you bought—it was a law stationer's shop; I know the technical name, because Fogo told me—you did not ask me to lend you the 6 s.; you did not tell me you were going to take out a writ against a man who owed you money—I did not ask to see the papers bought—I saw you make out some papers in a coffee shop—you did not say you were going to see Mrs. Spurway again before taking proceedings—I afterwards went with you to some square; I do not know where; you left me outside when you went in—I did not tell you to see Mrs. Spurway—I gave you the 8 s. at the Five Bells Tavern for a Judge's order—you told me you saw Mrs. Spurway overnight; you said she wished to put a stop to the proceedings; you said Mrs. Spurway proposed to pay 1 l. in fourteen days, and 5 s. a week until the debt and expenses were paid—I do not know that you said you could obtain a Judge's order upon that if she would consent—a consent to an older was not to be taken to Mrs. Spurway to sign; you did not say if she signed the consent you would require 8 s., the balance of the 30 s. you were to be paid—you came to my house next on Friday morning—I asked you what you meant by going to Mrs. Spurway, stating that you were Mr. Pawson's solicitor—I did not say to you that if you went to Mrs. Spurway she would pay you, or that she had sent to know if it would be all right for her to pay you—I was not at home when she sent to know that—I told you distinctly not to take the money of her, but to let it be paid into Court—Idid not tell you when you first went to Mrs. Spurway that I relied upon the amount of her debt to pay Pawson's bill; I merely said the goods were purchased at that warehouse—you did not tell me you had seen Mrs. Spurway on the Thursday night, and she had refused to sign the consent, or to pay the money by instalments—you told me the Judge's order was served upon her; you did net say she would pay at once if I reduced the debt—I saw you go into the house; I agreed to take off 1 l. when you came back you did not tell me she had paid 3 l. in discharge of the debt; you said you had made an arrangement that Mrs. Spurway was to pay 4 l. within eight days—I said, "You have taken off more than 1 l.; I did not intend to be more than one half, but never mind—you did not promise to call on the morning of Saturday to pay the money—I was at Greenwich on Monday; I went to the police court to know what I was to do, as I was quite sure you had done me out of my money—I was told to go to Fogo; I never asked you for the money—I told Wells on Saturday that I was to have the money on that day week, that it was to be paid into Court, and I was going with you on that day to get the money out.
COURT. Q. What did he say? A. He said he was very glad—I did not hear at all that Mrs. Spurway had paid, any money until afterwards—I never heard the prisoner say he had any—Mrs. Spurway told me she had paid.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not meet Wells on Sunday in Greenwich? A. Yes—he advised me to come to your house, and you would pay the money—Isaid, "No, I shall not"—I did not tell Wells I had seen Fogo, who urged me to give you into charge—I met you in the Railway Station, New Cross, on Wednesday—you did not say, "I may as well give you the money now, as wait until Saturday"—I did turn round, and run away towards Greenwich; that was for a policeman to give you into custody—you did not offer to pay the money.
MR. BAYLEY. Q. Did you ever see the prisoner before Saturday 13th Feb.? A. No, I did not—between the Saturday evening when he said, he had arranged with Mrs. Spurway and the following Wednesday when I gave him into charge I had only seen him once—I never agreed to give him 30 s. for what he was to do.
SUSANNAH NICHOLS . I am the wife of the last witness. I was present when the 15 s. were paid—no one else was present besides Wells—my husband said when he gave the 15 s., that was towards taking proceedings against Mrs. Spurway in the "Proctor's Court"—the prisoner said he would soon get the money; he said the expense would be 30 s. to recover the debt—theprisoner used the term "Proctor's Court," and said it must be recovered there.
Prisoner. Q. Did I use the words, "Proctor's Court?" A. Yes; you said, as she, had not administered, you must take the proceedings.
JANE SARAH SPURWAY . I am a widow, living near New Cross. On Monday,15th Feb., or Tuesday, the prisoner came about a debt that was owing by my late husband to Mr. Nichols—he said he called for the debt—I said he had sent two or three men before, and I had been so recently left I could not pay then, that he must wait; I said also, he sent in his bill and demanded it while my husband lay dead and uncoffined, and threatened he would take proceedings, and that if he were cruel, enough to do so he must have the trouble, for I had been left without auything—there were two persons who came before the prisoner—I saw the prisoner on the following Thursday, I paid him 1 l.; I afterwards paid him 2 l.—he read to me a Judge's order, with Mr. Nichols' name signed at the bottom—I do not know his handwriting—the prisoner told me he had signed it—he did not say proceedings were begun, but they would be—I did not authorize anybody to give my consent to a Judge's order; I did not know what it meant—except that piece of paper, I was not served with any proceedings—I never made a proposal to pay 1 l. in fourteen days, and the rest at 5 s. a week—I expressed a wish to have the proceedings stopped, and would settle if they would take less—I never mentioned any terms.
Prisoner. Q. Did not Mr. Nichols send two or three men to you? A. Yes—Ideclined to pay or make any arrangement—they were very impertinent—Mr. Nichols was present when one of them made an insulting speech—I said to you I had not administered, and he could not maker me pay—you said if I did not pay he would, take proceedings against me—I said I would see my father, and if you would call to-morrow I would consider it—you said on Wednesday evening, it would be wiser to pay by instalments; you mentioned the terms—when you next called I told you I had not seen my father—on Thursday you read a paper to me, it was a Judge's order—I did not know that you said if I paid 1 l. down, and 5 s. a week, and signed that paper, a Judge's order could be got—I said "If he will reduce the debt I will pay it at once"—the next morning when I paid you the money, I saw you go over to Mr. Nichols; I had then paid you 2 l.—I remember your saying Mr. Nichols depended upon the bill to pay Pawsons—you did not say you were Pawsons' attorney—I asked you, and you said you were not.
JOHN ALLEN ( Policeman, R 140). I apprehended the prisoner on 24th Feb.—he was in the County Court—the plaintiff was there, and he charged him in my presence with obtaining money by false pretences—he turned round, and said, "Now you have begun; by G—, I will serve you out"—he said at the police station, he collected debts for people.
Prisoner's Defence. I was engaged by Messrs. Miller and Son to serve
Nichols with a subpoena; I did so; he asked me for his expenses; I told him I was only a clerk, but if he looked on the back of the subpoena, he would see the address, and if he called there they would pay him; when I was attending the trial, he spoke to me about a debt; I told him I woould call and see what could be done; he agreed to give me 30 s. for my trouble; I told him a solicitor would be required, as he would have to administer, and he could not make her pay without; he told me if I called on Tuesday, he would let me have 15 s.—I called on Mrs. Spurway; she said she was not liable; I told her if she did not pay, proceedings would be taken against her; Nichols and I went to London, and I prepared an affidavit of the; debt being due; I then asked him to lend me 6 s. on account of the 30 s. I was to have, because I wanted to issue a writ against a; person who owed, me 11 l., and I had no money with me; I called on Mrs. Spurway again, and suggested she should pay by instalments of 5 s. a week; I saw Nichols, who said he would take it in that way; I said it would be necessary for him to sign a consent, and if Mrs. Spurway would sign it, a Judge's order could be taken out; I called afterwards again, and Mrs. Spurway said if Nichols would reduce the debt, she would pay at once; Nichols agreed to reduoe half the expenses; I took 3 l., and Nichols said he was sorry I took off the 5 s. 3 d., as I should have had that for my extra trouble; I said I was quite satisfied, and the widow would have the benefit; I saw Nichols at the New Cross station, and I told him I would pay the monay then, he ran away, I went after him to pay, I saw him and Fogo talking, and then he gave me into custody.
ROBERT WELLS . I remember seeing Nichols on the morning of the trial of Ashton v. Steel—he spoke to me about some debts owing to him, and of Mrs. Spurway's—he asked me if I knew any one who could get them in—I told him, I knew the prisoner had done similar transactions for other parties—I met him on Tuesday morning, he said, he was going down to Mr. Mart's house; would I go with him—he said, "He has arranged with Mrs. Spurway, and he wants 15 s., would it be right to give it?"—I said, I had no doubt it would be all right—I went down with him to the prisoner's house—he agreed to pay him 30 s., and asked if that would be sufficient—the 30 s. was for any trouble he might have in getting Mrs. Spurway's money—hesaid, "I will give you 15 s. down to-day;" that was to be in part of the 30 s.—I did not hear any thing said about the Proctor's Court—on Friday I called on Nichols, I asked him how the affair was going on—he said, Mart had arranged with Mrs. Spurway, and he was perfectly satisfied with what had been done, and the money was to be paid over on the following Saturday.—Imet him on the Saturday afternoon, and he said, you had taken Mrs. Spurway's money—I said, "If there is anything wrong, Mr. Mart will make it all right; let us go together to him"—he said, "I have something better coming off on Wednesday; I shall not trouble myself"—he did not say, "I am done"—perhaps he might have said something to that effect; that he was dissatisfied—on Wednesday, I heard the prisoner was taken before the magistrate—I called on Nichols in the evening; I asked him how he was going on—he said, "Oh, it's all right, I have got him locked up; I shall want you to go to-morrow morning to swear that the 15 s. was paid to take out proceedings in the Proctor's Court"—I said "I shall do no such thing; I never heard any thing of the kind mentioned, the money was to be for Mr. Mart's trouble"—I never offered money to Nichols for Mr. Mart.
Cross-examined by MR. BAYLEY. Q. What are you? A. I am, thank God, an independent man, living upon the work of my young days—I have
lived in that way more than ten years—I live in Frauds Street, Deptford—Ihave known the prisoner three years; not very intimately—I recommended him to Nichols—I did not tell him he was a solicitor—I swear Nichols said, the 30 s. was to be for the prisoner's own trouble—I did not hear it said, that it was for his own trouble and the expenses—I was called as a witness before the magistrate—I adhere to the statement that the 30 s. was for his trouble—Mr. Nichols said, "If that is not enough, I will pay you more," and Mart said, "I shall be satisfied;" I adhere to that.
( The witness's deposition was here read.)—"I am a shoemaker. I called upon Mr. Nichols on Friday—he told me he was perfectly satisfied with what Mart had done; that the money was to be paid on the following week—Iwas present at Nichols' when he paid the prisoner 15 s. he was to have 15 s. more—I did not hear that Mart was to have this for his own trouble, or whether it was for legal expenses."
Q. How do you account for that statement; is that true that you swore? A. I say the 30 s. was to be paid—I never heard about any proceedings—I said that before the magistrate, and I say so now.
COURT. Q. What was it to be for? A. I don't know, definitely; I don't know what the 30 s. was for, only for labour—Nichols said, "I shall give you 15 s. down, part of the 30 s.—I cannot swear positively, that the 15 s. was in part—I am quite certain it is true that Nichols said, "I will give you 15 s. in part of the 30 s.—I do not recollect telling him that Mart had a great many things in hand; and was short of money—I have seen the prisoner two or three times a week.
WILLAM NICHOLS re-examined by MR. BAYLEY). Q. You saw Wells at Guildhall? A. I did—I had conversation with him; he told me Mart was a solicitor; he afterwards told me, Mairt had a great many things in hand, and was short of cash—he said he had known him a great many years, and it would be all right.
Prisoner. Q. Do you know that the magistrate took this prosecution out of Fogo's hands? A. He said I had got into bad hands, and he would take me out of them.
GUILTY .— Judgement Respited.

480. JOHN BROWN (25) and THOMAS HOPKINS (24) , Burglary in the dwelling house of John Woodward Barrett, and stealing 90 cigars,1 timepiece, and other articles, value 6 l., and 3 l. 8 s. 8 d. in money, his property.
EDWARD BURKE ( Policeman, M 214). I was on duty in Rotherhithe, on 8th March, about 2 o'clock in the morning I saw the two prisoners; they were followed by a sergeant of police—I stopped them, and asked Brown what he had about him; he said he had nothing—I felt his coat pocket, and found these cigars—I asked him where he got them; he said, "From the Red Cow, at Deptford"—he then said, "It is no use giving the worms any trouble, go down to the Black Horse"—he was taken to the station—there were twelve dozen cigars found on him, and 2 l. 11 s. 8 d.
CHARLES HODGSON ( Policeman, S 28). I followed the two prisoners that morning, and called to the last witness to stop them—I searched Hopkins, and found on him a cribbage board, a pack of cards, and a timepiece—these are them—I asked him what he had got; he said, "Nothing"—I opened his coat and found this timepiece—he was further searched, and this cribbage board and pack of cards were found—I went to the prosecutor's house, and found the cellar flap had been opened by a crow bar.
JOHN WOODWARD BARRETT . I keep the Black Horse, at Rotherhithe—I was awoke that morning, by the police, about 3 o'clock—I found my house had been entered by the cellar flap—it had been all safe at 12 o'clock on the previous evening—this timepiece is mine, it was on the kitchen mantel piece—this cribbage board and cigars were all right in the bar parlour—I missed 3 l. 8 s. in money altogether.
Brown's Defence. We were coming from Deptford, and saw this time piece, and took it up; as to saying, "The Black Horse," we did not; we did not know where it came from; as to calling the policemen worms, it is quite false.
BROWN — GUILTY .*— Four Years Penal Servitude.
HOPKINS — GUILTY .— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18580405-481]
481. HENRY WARBURTON (21), was indicted for burglary in the dwelling house of Henry Griggs, with intent to steal.
HENRY GRIGGS . I keep the British Queen, in East Street, Old Kent Road. On 18th March, I went up to my bed room, on the second floor, about a quarter or twenty minutes before 10 o'clock at night—I found the door shut; Iopened it and found the prisoner at my drawers—I said, "What are you doing here?" he said he thought it was the club room—I have a club room, but that is below—I gave the prisoner in custody—he resisted very much, and struck me in the throat—the things in the drawer were disturbed—theroom door was kept locked, and my wife had the key in her pocket—the prisoner was very violent—he threw me back on the bed, but I held him tight—Ileft him in the officer's hands.
Prisoner. Q. Where was I standing? A. At the drawers—there was money in the top and in the bottom drawer—there was money in the drawer which you had got open—the drawers were not locked.
ANN GRIGGS . I kept some money in the drawers in the bed room—I had the key, and I had locked the room door about two hours before the robbery—Ihad gone up to put two sovereigns in the drawer—I kept the money there—the drawers were not locked.
WILLIAM PLANK . I am a clerk. I was in the prosecutor's house on the night this happened—I heard the noise—I went up stairs, and found Mr. Griggs and the prisoner—I stayed there to assist him.
Prisoner. Q. where were we at the time you came up? A. On the landing at the top of the stairs, on the top floor.
JOHN WEST ( Policeman, P 185). I was called to the house, and found the prosecutor and the prisoner in the bed room—the prisoner was given into my charge—I asked him what he was doing there?—he said he thought it was a club room, and he had made a mistake—I found him three skeleton keys—one of them opened the bed room door—I examined the lock and tried the key, and broke it; I had it repaired, and it opened it quite easily.
Prisoner. Q. Do you know whether the lock had been opened before you came to it? A. I found the lock had been damaged.
WILLIAM BARNES . I am potman to the prosecutor—I heard a noise up stairs; I went up—I called for help—a person came up—I held the prisoner by the neck—he had got master down on the floor.
GUILTY .
He was further charged with having been before convicted: to which he
PLEADED GUILTY.— Four Years Penal Servitude.
